- `web/modules/page_header.js` renders common page headers/tab strips.
- `web/modules/page_icons.js` is the nav/header icon SSOT.
- `web/modules/composer_parts.js` is the ordered-composer-parts SSOT: the pure parts reducer (`pushText`/`pushChip`/`popLast`/`normalizeParts`, where `normalizeParts` is also the ONE gate every chip passes — a hand-assembled part with an unusable range degrades to the whole-file form rather than serializing a claim like `L5-L2` that nothing could parse back) plus the ONE reversible `[context: <path> L<a>-L<b>]` marker codec (`serializeParts`/`parseContent`, fence length = longest backtick run + 1, a selection over `MAX_CHIP_LINES` keeps its TRUE line range and drops only the inlined bytes so the agent reads exactly that span itself, a whole-file chip is the bare `[context: <path>]` marker, and a path that cannot round-trip is REFUSED at capture time rather than silently mangled, and `makeChipPart` holds the PRODUCER contract for captured bytes — the selected text verbatim, LF, no trailing newline, with CRLF and one trailing newline normalized there; the range and the bytes may not CONTRADICT each other, which is a FLOOR rather than an equality: a file capture fills its range exactly, a DIFF capture legitimately carries more (an interleaved `-` row is real selected text that the new-side range cannot number), and only content spanning FEWER lines than the range names is dropped, leaving the chip ranged-but-bare. What keeps the floor honest is that `chipLabel` counts the BYTES a chip hides and never the range it claims, so a folded chip cannot conceal a line), plus a thin `createComposerParts({container, input})` DOM mount over the same core. It carries no transport and no send logic — the serialized marker string is ordinary message text, so `contracts.py` and the system prompt are untouched. Every composer — the chat composer itself included — mounts this module, and the Changes / Files docks hand their ordered parts to the chat controller's `setDraftParts` / `sendParts`. Both entry points are loss-averse: `setDraftParts` ADDS to the chat field (whatever was already there, chips plus a half-typed sentence, is committed ahead of the incoming parts) and an empty handoff is a no-op, while `sendParts` resolves the REAL dispatch outcome from `sendMessage` (true only when the frame was delivered or deliberately queued) so a source dock clears its own draft on success and never after a refusal.
- `web/modules/api_client.js` is the frontend API boundary.
- `web/modules/api_types.js` mirrors browser-facing envelopes with JSDoc.
- `web/modules/ui_helpers.js` centralizes tone badges, age labels, inline status, host-bridge downloads, and the safe field renderer/value collector shared by Widgets and Settings (Settings retains its narrow route/component contract).
- `web/modules/skill_card_renderer.js` renders installed Skills cards from shared lifecycle/review/grant state.
- `web/modules/update_status.js` (v6.41.0) renders the main-screen Update pill + the staged auto/assisted/manual dialog; `web/modules/activity.js` (v6.41.0) renders the Dashboard Activity subtab (cron schedules, running/queued tasks, background consciousness) with direct mechanical controls. Both use the shared `.btn` button system.
- `web/modules/toast.js`, `masonry.js`, and CSS tokens in `style.css` keep cards/notifications/layout consistent without a build system.

Each active or deleting Project has a sidebar row, and its extra THREADS are listed in a sibling container directly beneath it (`web/modules/project_threads.js`) — a sibling, because a project row is one button and interactive rows may not be nested inside a button. The project row IS the project's thread #0: clicking it opens that chat, its kebab renames or deletes the project or forks its main thread, and its `+` creates a new thread (an empty chat sharing the project's folder). That kebab also carries thread #0's OWN branch/merge/changes/checkout rows, from the same `threadActionItemsHtml`/`runThreadAction` pair the thread rows use: without them A7 ("each thread works in the project folder OR in its own checkout") held for every thread EXCEPT the one the project opens by default, and nothing would ever have said so — the routes accept thread #0 perfectly well and no client was asking them. Archive and delete are deliberately NOT among them: thread #0 has no lifecycle of its own, the server refuses both by name, and `threadActions` renders that as a disabled row carrying the reason. Each listed thread has its own row, its own unread dot, and its own menu, mounted through the SAME accessible row-menu shell as the project row so there is one keyboard and placement behaviour, not two. That menu is Rename…/Fork plus the branch/merge/checkout/lifecycle rows built from `project_thread_actions.threadActions` (`threadActionItemsHtml`), so "what may this thread do right now" has ONE definition and the menu renders it rather than restating it. A row that cannot run is DISABLED carrying its reason as the tooltip, never omitted — a missing item teaches nothing, a greyed one teaches what to do first — and the thread's LOCATION is asked once per menu open (`GET …/worktree`, which also carries the inspection the removal prompt needs), because a location is derived and never stored (A7); a read that FAILS disables the rows that depend on it and says so rather than reporting "works in the project folder" for a checkout it could not see. `runThreadAction` owns what each answer MEANS and is the ONE place a thread gesture calls `threadOps` — the archived-threads menu's Restore included, which used to await `ops.restore` directly: a typed 409 was handled (the envelope unwraps to a value), but a 500, an HTML error page or a transport error re-threw past `onSelect` into an async click listener with no try/catch, so the owner saw nothing, nothing refreshed, and the stale archived row stayed clickable (P2). Do not await a `threadOps` call outside it. and every refusal that carries an owner-answerable part is offered its second call in the same gesture: `checkout_dirty` on merge-back and `checkout_holds_rebuildable_files` on delete re-send with the acknowledgement after naming exactly what stays behind, T2's typed `git_init_required` `decision` is rendered as the `init_git` offer whose yes is `apiClient.projectInitGit` and whose success retries the original gesture, and `checkout_holds_work` is deliberately NOT answerable here — its way out is a merge back or an acknowledged removal. `merge_abort_failed` gets its OWN banner rather than one more red sentence: a conflict left the folder byte-for-byte as it was and this did not, so the copy names the folder and the `git merge --abort` the owner has to run there themselves. A branch-off receipt discloses BOTH sensitive-file facts (`skipped_sensitive` — kept out of the commit, still untracked in the folder; `tracked_sensitive` — already tracked, so committed with everything else), which is the only surface the second one has. `ask`/`ops` are injected with production defaults purely so these decisions are testable under `node --test`, where the DOM dialog cannot run (the seam is `ask`, not `confirm`: that is the banned native dialog's name and the deterministic gate over `web/modules` cannot tell a parameter from the global). One thing had to change on the T3 side for ANY of it to run in a browser: a typed refusal arrives as a THROW, not a return value — the routes answer 400/404/409 and `api_client.fetchJson` rejects on every non-2xx — so `threadOps` now unwraps the envelope off `error.body` (`typedAnswer`) and hands it back as data. Unwrapped it was unreachable end to end: `describeOutcome` never saw a refusal, `acknowledgeable` never rendered its second call, and every escape T3 built read to the owner as "something went wrong". A body that is NOT one of those envelopes — a 500, an HTML error page, a dropped connection — still throws, because dressing an outage up as a refusal tells the owner their work was declined when nothing decided anything. THREE lifecycles reach a row and are painted apart (`threadRowPresentation`): `active`, `deleting` (the deliberate end of a deletion that would not quiesce — greyed, labelled, not draggable, no unread dot, and the ONLY action it offers is `Retry delete` carrying `thread.delete_error` as the reason it is offered) and `archived` (which reaches the sidebar only while a task is still live in it, X10 — italic and labelled, still openable, because the point of showing it is the output it is still producing). An archive that answers `visible_until_terminal` SAYS so: the route documents that flag as existing "so the surface can say which of the two just happened", and until the menu read it the owner archived a thread, watched it stay exactly where it was, and had no way to tell a deliberate rule from an instruction that did not land. ARCHIVED threads are otherwise invisible to the sidebar, which paints `/api/state` and whose projection filters them, so the project row menu carries `Archived threads…` — `apiClient.projectsList(true)` → `?include_archived=1`, listed in a second row menu whose selection calls `restore`. Without that one affordance `restore` was unreachable by construction and archiving was a one-way trip with a documented inverse nobody could press. The backend owns the 80-character name limit and lifecycle truth.

Ordering is deliberately not clever: a new project sits on top of the list, a new thread on top within its project, and threads stay grouped under the project they belong to. The owner can drag either level into any order; that manual order is an explicit PREFIX persisted through the same `/api/ui/preferences` surface `widget_order` uses (`project_order`, `project_thread_order`), and anything not placed by hand keeps the default order behind it. There is no unread-based hoisting — a list that reshuffles itself when a message arrives is the moving-target problem manual ordering exists to end. A stale id in a stored order is ignored rather than scrambling the rest.

Opening a thread renders its chat in the CENTRE area, the same place Main Chat lives, and closing it returns to Main. It is NOT a right-side panel: that model turned into a second full-screen overlay stacked on the content area at phone width, with two competing close affordances and two full-screen surfaces. The right panel is now the task inspector's alone, so a thread and an inspected task can be open together. A deleting Project becomes non-openable and visibly remains in the transitional state until the server publishes authoritative registry state. On narrow screens navigation becomes an explicit drawer; there is no gesture-only navigation layer competing with message scroll, text selection, or the software keyboard.

At most ONE thread chat instance is live (`web/app.js`), keyed by project AND thread — keying it by project alone would make two threads of one project share an instance, a scroll stash and a paint receipt, and would let opening a sibling thread discard the other thread's staged attachments. The one exception remains an instance holding unsendable client state, which is hidden and marked rather than destroyed.

Shared frontend primitives prevent pages from acquiring competing contracts. `page_header.js` owns page headers and tab strips; `page_icons.js` owns navigation/header icons; `api_client.js` owns browser API calls and typed error propagation; `api_types.js` mirrors the browser-facing contract shapes; `ui_helpers.js` owns shared status, safe-field, and host-bridge behavior; `skill_card_renderer.js` owns installed-skill cards; `log_events.js` owns event classification and task/review presentation shared by Chat and Logs; `toast.js`, `masonry.js`, and CSS tokens own common notifications and layout. The reason is dependency control: frontend work should not require reimplementing supervisor, review, marketplace, extension, and provider semantics in each page.

`confirm_dialog.js::openConfirmDialog` is the one browser-dialog authority. Confirm mode resolves a strict boolean; input mode resolves `{confirmed, value}` and returns an empty value on cancellation; alert mode renders one acknowledgement button. Cancel, Close, backdrop, Escape, and supersession by a newer dialog all resolve as non-confirmation. Native `window.prompt`, `window.confirm`, and `window.alert` are forbidden in `web/modules`: they are visually and behaviorally inconsistent across shells, block the browser event loop, and `window.prompt` silently returns `null` in the macOS PyWebView shell because that backend has no prompt delegate. Critical controls therefore act only on the exact confirmed result; Panic's confirm-and-send sequence is one testable operation rather than a confirmation call detached from the command it guards.

### Chat and Projects

`web/modules/chat.js` owns the canonical message timeline, input recall and draft, attachment staging, runtime controls, budget projection, routing annotations, task cards, child cards, delivered media, and reconnect reconciliation. Every ordinary message has one canonical durable chat row. Project views are lenses over those rows and task bindings; Project conversion does not create a second message, unread event, or cost record. Routing acknowledgements live in a compact sidecar keyed by `client_message_id` and update the existing owner message without adding a synthetic assistant bubble.

`web/modules/chat.js` owns the message timeline, input, attachment staging, input recall, runtime controls, and live task cards. The header carries the global agent controls as ghost buttons — Evolve, Review, Restart — plus one danger Panic (confirm-gated) and a slim `More` `<details>` menu holding Consciousness (auto-dismissed on outside click / Escape); its live-mode state comes from the shared `/api/state` snapshot, not a chat-local poll. The budget meter is NOT in the header: `headerBudgetPresentation` remains the one budget formatting projection (fail-closed "Unavailable", never a fabricated `$0`) and the sidebar meter is its only consumer. `initChat`'s returned controller is KEPT by `web/app.js` (`getChatController()`) and exposes `setDraftParts(parts)` / `sendParts(parts)`: other screens hand ORDERED composer parts to the chat through that seam, which serializes them and rides the parts editor + the existing `sendMessage` transport — the serialized string stays the message identity for WS frames, history, dedup, and recall. It loads persisted history from `/api/chat/history`, merges echoed local messages by `client_message_id`, and collapses task/progress/tool chatter into expandable cards rather than transcript spam. Every top-level bubble, media item, and task-card root carries numeric epoch `data-ts` derived from the raw source timestamp before display formatting. `insertMessageNode` inserts before the first sibling with a strictly greater timestamp, preserves arrival order for equal timestamps, leaves timestamp-free nodes on the append path, and always keeps the typing indicator last; an insertion above a scrolled-up viewport compensates `scrollTop` by the inserted `scrollHeight` delta, while a reconnect rebuild restores the first visible timestamped DOM anchor (so simultaneous older rows above and new rows below cannot move the reader), and near-bottom insertion retains normal autoscroll. A task card anchors its earliest raw timestamp on its DOM root, so ordinary card recreation naturally starts a fresh anchor without separate reset state. A logical chat message has one canonical `chat.jsonl` row; Project conversion stores a source ref and the history endpoint projects that row into the Project lens rather than creating a mirror bubble, duplicate unread, or duplicate cost. Owner-visible routing status is a compact `chat_annotations.jsonl` sidecar keyed by `client_message_id`; it is presentation-only. Chat attachments are staged client-side from paperclip, paste, and chat-wide file drag/drop, capped at 10 files, 50 MB per file, and 100 MB total per message; upload happens only immediately before send, attachment messages bypass the offline WebSocket queue, and partial upload/send failures best-effort DELETE already uploaded temporary files while preserving the staged batch for retry. The composer is a FLAT 12px box (`--bg-elevated` + 1px border + accent focus ring — no blur anywhere in the SPA) whose text surface is a `composer_parts` mount: the field is an ORDERED sequence of context chips and committed text followed by the live input, so `⌘L` captures interleave with typed comments and `sendMessage` reads the whole field through the ONE shared serializer. A control row above it carries Swarm, Low/Max and a READ-ONLY model chip (the main model from the Settings module's already-fetched `/api/settings` snapshot, refreshed on `ouro:settings-updated`, click opens Settings → Models; hidden entirely until that snapshot exists rather than showing a guessed default), while Send stays inside the text row. `sendMessage` resolves TRUE only on a real dispatch and FALSE on every bail-out (refusal, offline attachment, failed upload, lost connection, nothing to send), and `_pendingCardObjective` is derived from the HUMAN text parts only — a serialized `[context:]` marker is machine grammar, never a project title (it is re-armed on EVERY main-chat send, an empty objective included, so a chips-only message cannot inherit the previous message's title). Input recall addresses the WHOLE FIELD, not the live input alone: an entry is the serialized string that was sent, so it is read back through the codec and its chips return as CHIPS (the trailing text part stays in the live input so recalled words remain editable), and the un-recalled draft is snapshotted with the same serializer. Recall holds the same LOSSLESS-OR-RAW invariant as the transcript: an entry the codec cannot round-trip byte for byte is placed in the live input VERBATIM with nothing committed as parts, so ArrowUp-then-send transmits the identical string instead of a shorter re-serialization. Subagent progress uses separate child cards keyed by `subagent_task_id`/`task_id`; parent cards receive lineage references (`parent_task_id`, `root_task_id`, child id, role) without duplicating child bubbles on reload/reconnect. Ordinary nested child cards stay visible but collapsed by default with role-first headings so deep trees remain scannable; a child carrying review evidence expands by default so actor/model/verdict provenance is disclosed immediately. Each card is its own inline-size container: narrow summaries and timeline controls wrap against the card's actual width, and nesting indentation flattens after the first level so deep trees retain a usable text column. Mobile keyboard handling lives in `web/app.js` + CSS `keyboard-open` classes. Keyboard mode requires a focused editable plus a visual-viewport shrink from the last stable application viewport; opening the drawer blurs that editable and clears the keyboard state before navigation is shown, so sidebar state, backdrop, and rendered visibility cannot diverge.

The transcript is FLAT (flat-UI redesign): one centred 760px reading column, in which the owner's message is the only bubble (right-aligned, `12 12 4 12` radius) and everything else is a plain row. An agent turn — whether a single reply or a live task card — opens with the same sender line, so a run reads as the same voice rather than a boxed widget: sender, a pulsing phase pill, and mono meta carrying the REAL fields the frames supplied (phase, round, sticky cost, latest-activity clock). There is deliberately no "step N of M": no such field exists. Task activity is a mono log behind a 2px left rule, one row per event, with a `✓`/`●`/`✕` status glyph rendered from the row's phase class as a `::before` (no extra markup, so every `data-live-line-*` disclosure contract is untouched) and the event's real timestamp right-aligned — there is no per-row duration in the frames and none is invented. Subagents are an indented flat GROUP behind their own rule, keeping the first-level-only indentation flattening. Row titles WRAP instead of ellipsizing: a CSS ellipsis on owner-facing narration would hide cognitive text with no disclosure (BIBLE P1). Only the transient, non-persisted status events (awakened / reconnected) render as a centred pill; every rich system renderer — `skill_review` above all, with its full expandable text and the `.ui-rich-content` contract — keeps its complete disclosure. An owner message renders its `[context:]` markers as compact chips, in order, through the ONE pure `renderUserContent(raw)`: the RAW serialized string stays the message identity everywhere (WS frame, `chat.jsonl`, dedup, recall), the captured bytes ride the payload the agent reads rather than being pasted back at the owner, and anything that is not an exactly-formed marker stays escaped text. The composer previews its parts inline (chips sitting before the live input in the same field); the transcript is a different surface with the same source of truth — it stacks the parts line per line, exactly as the payload bytes separate them, rather than reproducing the composer's layout. The projection is LOSSLESS-OR-RAW: chips are shown only when `serializeParts(parseContent(raw))` reproduces the message byte for byte, and any message it cannot (a fence longer than `MAX_CHIP_LINES`, whose bytes the serializer legitimately drops) is rendered as the WHOLE raw string in escaped text — every line visible, nothing folded — instead of collapsing hundreds of lines the agent still receives into one marker line. Within a lossless projection every chip's label counts the BYTES it hides, not the range it claims (`chipLabel`), so folding one can never conceal a line: a marker over a five-line fence says "5 lines" whatever range it names. The codec's floor is what makes that safe — a fence may carry MORE lines than its range names, because a diff capture inlines the selected diff rows verbatim and an interleaved `-` row is real selected text the new-side range cannot number, but never FEWER, since there the range would overclaim what the fence can show and the bytes are dropped instead (which the lossless-or-raw check above then renders whole). Segments are joined by the same newline that separates them in the serialized string. Live root cards in the MAIN chat also carry an "Inspect" action that dispatches the `ouro:inspect-task` `{taskId}` contract and nothing else — the card holds no inspector knowledge, so with no listener registered the action is simply a no-op. It is deliberately a main-chat affordance in v1; since a thread moved to the centre the inspector no longer competes with it, so extending the action to thread cards is a scoping decision rather than a layout constraint. A reconnect REBUILD owns scroll position for its duration: while the column is emptied and refilled, `isNearBottom()` would answer about a transiently near-empty transcript, so stickiness consumers are suppressed and the explicit decision at the end of `syncHistory` (restore the reader's DOM anchor, or stick only if they really were at the bottom BEFORE the rebuild) is the sole authority.

Top-level messages, media bubbles, and task-card roots are ordered by their raw numeric timestamps rather than formatted display text. An insertion precedes only siblings with a strictly later timestamp, so equal timestamps retain arrival order and timestamp-free transient nodes retain append order; the typing indicator remains last. Inserts and synchronous card-height changes share a stable-viewport boundary: a reader near the bottom stays pinned, while a reader examining earlier content keeps the visible message or nested-card anchor at the same screen offset. Scroll position is remembered per chat instance and restored after a Project panel is recreated.

History reconciliation is two-pass. Progress and system records first rebuild timestamped task-card state; cards and regular user/assistant messages are then inserted chronologically; only after that may terminal state seal a card. This prevents terminal replay from discarding earlier progress, preserves progress-only and nested-child cards, and recovers cards whose final summary was missed while disconnected. Live echoes and history rows are deduplicated with stable message identities, while a reconnect may still rebuild ordinary bubbles from durable history when the prior socket missed them.

The Main chat receives ordinary main-thread dialogue and may mirror Project progress, digests, and logs as a headquarters view; it does not duplicate raw Project user and assistant dialogue. Project panels accept only their own registered `chat_id`. The complete Project chat-id set is separate from the sidebar's visible or bounded summary, so a file-less, inactive, or currently off-list Project cannot have an early frame misclassified as Main. A `projects_changed` frame adds the new chat id synchronously before the asynchronous state refresh.

A Project owns one or more THREADS — each an empty chat sharing the project's working folder. Thread #0 IS the project's existing chat: `thread_chat_id(pid, 0) == project_chat_id(pid)` exactly, so every history row, task binding, unread counter and test keeps its numbers and threads need no migration. Extra threads live in an additive `threads: []` list on the registry row; the canonical read-time projection (`project_threads_registry.project_threads`, re-exported from `projects_registry`) synthesizes thread #0 from the project's own chat id, name, creation time and its OWN `thread0_visible_revision`, and the top-level `chat_id` remains its compatibility alias. Chat ids are deterministic hashes with no allocator, so uniqueness is a REGISTRY-WIDE reservation invariant instead: every project and thread creation and the boot reconcile check the minted id against every id every project and thread (in any lifecycle, tombstones included) already owns; a thread simply retries the next opaque thread id, a project collision is refused loudly, and pre-existing duplicates are detected on load and reported loudly (once per registry version, so a collision hand-edited in later is still surfaced). Forking a thread stores only a `{fork_of_chat_id, fork_before_ts}` cursor — the parent's rows are never copied, so a fork mints no new row identities, no second consolidation and no extra rotation pressure — and the cursor keeps resolving whether the parent is alive, archived or deleted. Reading a fork therefore means reading its own chat plus a bounded slice of every ancestor, and that slice has exactly ONE definition: `ouroboros/thread_history.py::thread_ancestry_lens`, consumed by both the history endpoint and the agent's focused project context, so neither surface can define the shared past differently from the other. That is a claim about the DEFINITION, not about coverage, and the difference is load-bearing: the two READERS are not the same, so the same lens can still yield different histories, in exactly two named ways and no others. (1) The origin-fallback synthesis below is on the history surface only. (2) `context.py` filters ONE bounded tail of the shared live `chat.jsonl` (`_PROJECT_THREAD_SCAN`) while the history endpoint reads per-thread with archive backfill, so on a busy install an ancestor's rows fall out of that tail before the lens sees them and the agent gets less than the owner (P8). Going and fetching them per ancestor across archives is deliberately NOT done here — it overlaps §C+'s deferred archive-cap work — so it is DISCLOSED instead: the focused context carries a `## Conversation gaps in this view` section naming the parent chats it applies to and saying the owner's history reaches further back. The test is PER ANCESTOR and the cap is necessary but not sufficient — either the oldest scanned row is newer than that ancestor's cutoff (none of its window is in view), or the scan hit its cap AND shows no sign of having reached back past that ancestor's beginning (it holds none of that ancestor's admitted rows, or the oldest one it holds is the oldest row the scan read at all). The cap ALONE used to be the whole test, which put the section on every fork on any install whose live journal has reached 4000 rows — permanently, and including forks whose entire shared past sits inside the window with thousands of older rows read behind it; a disclosure that is always on is one the reader learns to skip. The residual is named rather than silent: an ancestor SILENT across the oldest rows of the window and active before it is not detected, because telling that apart needs a read below the window, which is the same deferred archive-cap work this section discloses instead of doing. An earlier version of this sentence claimed the two can NEVER differ and named one exception; it was false in two places. Cutoffs are inclusive at the fork instant, intersected along a fork-of-fork chain, and carry the ancestors' binding source refs. The ancestor ORIGIN-FALLBACK synthesis — the projection that makes a fork of a CONVERTED project still show the Main-chat message that started it — is currently on the HISTORY surface only (`gateway/history.py::_origin_fallback_rows`); `context.py` shares the lens and therefore the same ancestors and cutoffs, but not that synthesis, so the AGENT working in such a fork can still miss that one projected row. Named rather than implied, because the point of the shared lens is that every divergence between the two surfaces is a NAMED one (carry-forward T0R2-8; it is deliberately sequenced behind the fix to that synthesis's dedupe-before-cutoff ordering, T0R2-10, so the shared version is lifted once and already correct). An ancestor is followed ONLY when `resolve_chat_binding` recognises it as a thread of the SAME project — a hand-written `fork_of_chat_id` would otherwise pour the whole Main conversation, or another project's conversation, into a thread — and a cycle that closes on the REQUESTING chat never tightens that chat's own cutoff. A chain deeper than the ancestry cap, a cycle in hand-edited state, or an ancestor that is unbound or bound to a DIFFERENT project sets a `truncated` flag that `/api/chat/history` reports as the `ancestry_depth` window cause, rather than quietly serving a short history. "The registry could not be READ" is a THIRD state and never wears the ancestor-less answer's clothes: `_chat_binding` fails closed to `None` (as against `{}`, which means the registry answered and this chat owns no thread), and that sets `lens_unavailable` alongside `truncated`, reported as its own window cause and as a `## Conversation gaps in this view` section in the agent's context. Folding the two together is how a fork lost its ENTIRE ancestry with no exception raised anywhere, while `_window_metadata` still answered `complete: True` and the agent's focused context silently narrowed to its own rows (P6). Activity in any thread advances that thread's OWN visible revision and the project aggregate the flat `project_seen_revision` cursor reads; thread #0 keeps its own counter (`thread0_visible_revision`, seeded from the aggregate on a legacy row) so a sibling thread's message does not mark the project's main thread unread.

The Main composer exposes one-shot Swarm planning, the owner Low/Max context choice, file attachment, and Send. Swarm places a structural `force_plan` fact on the next ordinary message and disarms after that send; it is not inferred from keywords. Low/Max uses the dedicated owner endpoint. An automatically derived Low fit never masquerades as an owner-selected Low posture, and selecting Max may invoke the existing exact-route capability acknowledgement when provider metadata cannot prove the requested window. Project panels intentionally omit global Restart, Panic, evolution, consciousness, review, and budget controls because those belong to the one Ouroboros process, not to an individual Project thread.

Files can be staged with the paperclip, pasted images, or drag-and-drop over the chat. One message accepts at most ten files, 50 MB per file, and 100 MB in total. Staging is local and upload begins only immediately before Send. Attachment messages are refused while offline instead of entering the ordinary in-memory WebSocket queue: queued attachment references could outlive or orphan their temporary uploads. If a partial upload or final socket send fails, already uploaded temporary files are deleted best-effort and the original staged batch remains available for retry. Structured attachment metadata lets the gateway expose a native image to a vision-capable route and stage the complete file set through the shared artifact substrate.

Simple text messages may appear immediately as pending local bubbles and reconcile against their echoed `client_message_id`. Delivered documents use a durable download URL where available and rebuild from history without persisting base64; the desktop shell opens or downloads them through the host bridge so WKWebView is not navigated into a file, while a real browser uses a fetch/blob fallback. Live photo and video frames render as media bubbles. Presentation must not imply that a socket write is durable acceptance: routing and terminal truth come from host receipts, durable chat history, task records, and subsequent state reconciliation.

Task activity is collapsed into a live task card per root instead of flooding the transcript with individual tool and progress bubbles. `log_events.js` keeps the live task card and grouped task cards on one reducer across Chat and Dashboard Logs. A card keeps a concise latest activity line and an expandable chronological timeline; server-truncated result or trace rows fetch their complete typed task record on demand into a bounded viewer. The compact card is therefore a navigation/presentation projection, not the full result authority.

Subagents render as distinct child cards keyed by their actual child task ids. Parent cards retain lineage references without duplicating the child's final answer into the parent timeline. Nested children are collapsed by default to keep deep trees scannable, while review evidence remains expanded when hiding it would conceal actor, model, route, verdict, or capability provenance. Requested model lane, effective model lane, delegated executor route, and terminal execution evidence remain separate fields: a dispatch intention must not be redrawn as proof of where the run actually settled.

Card cost is sticky task-scope evidence. Only frames that carry task accounting status, finality, subtree, reservation, or unknown-cost fields may update it; an unrelated per-call `cost_usd` delta is never relabelled as the task total. The precedence is unavailable, then pending, then final, with newer evidence winning within one class. Costless heartbeat or rendering frames cannot erase a known value, and a transient unavailable read cannot overwrite a later honest measurement. Dashboard accounting and task-detail accounting continue to derive from the physical-attempt ledger rather than from the card.

A Cancel action appears only on unfinished, unconverted, pooled root cards carrying the supervisor's host-attested `cancelable=true`; card shape alone is insufficient because direct in-process chat turns can look identical but have no queue entry. Chat and Activity ask for explicit confirmation and call the typed task endpoint with `cascade:true`, meaning the root and its live descendant subtree. The endpoint returns only after teardown or a typed refusal. Natural completion wins a race, a missing live task is reconciled from the durable record, and a process that cannot be proven dead remains a visible refusal rather than being painted Cancelled.

A Main root card may be turned into a Project. Conversion creates or reuses the Project, gives it an owner-facing name without asking for an internal task id, binds the task and its canonical origin message, moves the live work onto the Project lane, and replaces the Main action with a calm Project pointer. Follow-up tasks already bound to a Project never receive another conversion button. Naming reuses an already coined model title when available and otherwise falls back through the current server naming path; the UI does not invent a second name authority.

The New Project dialog supports exactly one source: no folder for research/chat, a fresh managed genesis workspace, an attached existing folder, or a cloned Git URL. Attach uses a server-side directory browser so the flow also works in ordinary web and Docker environments. A non-git attached folder is rejected unless the owner explicitly requests the attach-snapshot initialization; it is never initialized silently. Clone failures distinguish missing credentials. Attach, clone, and genesis disclose that Project tasks receive read, write, and shell access in the chosen folder, and provenance remains a durable historical fact rather than being recomputed from current git state.

Deleting a Project is lifecycle work, not filesystem deletion. The server first fences new admission, then cancels and quiesces the Project task subtree, and finally tombstones the registry entry. The UI may acknowledge that deletion started and show the transitional state, but it does not claim completion early. Project id, canonical chat history, task bindings, memory, provenance, and the working folder remain preserved; deleting the row is not permission to erase the owner's repository or the agent's history.

Unread state is PER THREAD and durable: a thread is unread exactly when its own `visible_revision` exceeds its own entry in the nested `project_seen_revision` cursor (`{project_id: {thread_id: revision}}` — see §11.4 for the flat→nested migration). A project ROW in the sidebar owns no unread number of its own: its dot is thread #0's own unread state, nothing more, so a sibling thread's message can never light it and can never mark the project's main thread read. The GROUP aggregate — unread threads summed across every project — is the `#nav-projects-count` pill in the Projects header, which is exactly the number that survives collapsing the list. Only owner-visible assistant/result content, delivered media, or a real incident advances a visible revision; ordinary progress and heartbeat traffic do not. Opening a thread does not clear its unread by itself. The browser refreshes and paints that exact thread history revision, verifies that the thread is still the visible one and still connected, then posts that revision as the acknowledgement. The server clamps it to that THREAD's current truth (thread #0 against `thread0_visible_revision`, never the project aggregate) and max-merges it with the stored cursor, so a stale tab cannot move the cursor backwards or acknowledge future output.

A chat instance has an explicit resource lifecycle. `ws.on()` registers listeners in insertion-ordered sets and returns a disposer; event emission iterates a snapshot so adding or removing a listener during dispatch cannot corrupt neighboring delivery. `destroy()` marks the instance dead, disposes all socket subscriptions, removes window/document listeners, disconnects its observer, clears history, header, and task timers, drops bounded in-memory collections, and removes the DOM last. Late animation frames, history requests, preference reads, full-result fetches, and paint acknowledgements check the destroyed flag.

`app.js` normally keeps at most one live Project chat instance. Closing or switching stashes only its scroll intent and destroys the instance. The narrow exception is unsendable client state: staged `File` objects or an upload already in flight. Such an instance is hidden and marked pending instead of destroyed, is reused if the Project is reopened, and returns to the ordinary destroy policy after that work settles. Typed but unsent text survives separately in per-thread session storage. This prevents hidden Project rooms from accumulating listeners, repainting, or acknowledging unseen revisions without discarding data the server cannot reconstruct.

Chat and progress logs rotate as one timeline and archived segments remain durable. Interactive history uses bounded, archive-aware readers that expand only far enough to satisfy the requested thread's filtered quota; Project history cannot be satisfied by unrelated Main rows. Terminal annotation occurs after the emitted window is chosen, and display reads avoid materializing or rebasing artifacts. The task-event stream performs an archive-aware replay and then follows appended bytes, handling rotation and newly discovered children; its terminal event performs the one materializing task read needed to deliver artifact-bearing final truth. Children are discovered by a scandir name-diff over the main root's task-results directory — the subagent scheduler durably writes a child's result file there, with lineage and child drive, before the child is enqueued or emits events — so a follow tick decodes only result files it has not successfully read yet instead of re-projecting the whole store; the disclosed residual is that the per-tick directory scan itself stays proportional to the size of the task-results directory. The reason is bounded UI latency without treating log rotation as conversation loss or mutating artifact state merely to render status.

### Changes and the task inspector

`web/modules/changes.js` (`#page-changes`) is the diff-review screen: a task rail from `GET /api/tasks` (newest first, capped at 30 — a review surface, not a task log; the project badge is the task's OWN `project_id` first and the `/api/state.task_bindings` post-hoc binding second, names from the already-fetched project summaries, falling back to the raw id so a project beyond the capped sidebar list is still labelled), a file list, and unified / split renderers over the same parsed hunks. `web/modules/task_inspector.js` registers the `inspector` right-panel kind against the app-level state machine (mutually exclusive with any other registered kind, fixed `--inspector-width`, mobile overlay; a project thread lives in the centre and does not compete for the slot), opens on the `ouro:inspect-task` event a live card dispatches, and carries exactly two tabs — Changes (file rows → the full Changes screen) and Cost — plus a footer of `+N −N`, task cost and elapsed. The footer's counts read `—`, not `+0 −0`, unless a READY diff is behind them: `+0 −0` is the claim "this task changed nothing", which is the wrong claim for a diff that is pending, blocked, or not fetched yet. Its Cost tab renders ONLY fields the task record actually persists (accounted cost with its accounting status/final flag, rounds, prompt/completion tokens, reserved, unresolved upper bound, unknown-unmetered) under the same fail-closed discipline as the chat card's `taskCostMeta`: an unreadable ledger reads "Unavailable", never a convincing `$0.00`. Elapsed reads the durable `duration_sec` scalar — the SAME value the `task_metrics` event already carries (one timing, two consumers) — so a task whose live event stream the browser never saw still shows an honest elapsed instead of a fabricated zero. Scope worth stating exactly: `duration_sec` is persisted by the pipeline's terminal writer (`agent_task_pipeline._store_task_result`, i.e. the completed and failed paths); the cancel and crash writers omit the key, and the inspector shows "Unavailable" for those rather than inventing a zero. The inspector's live refresh rides the ONE app-owned `/api/state` poll and stops once the task is terminal, and that tick re-reads the task RECORD only: the diff endpoint forks git on the server, so the diff is fetched when the answer can actually change — panel open, Changes-tab activation, and once on the task's terminal transition — never a few times a minute per open panel.

`GET /api/tasks/{task_id}/diff` answers ONE typed diff projection `{status, source, base_commit, head_advanced, blockers[], patch, patch_sha256}` for the Changes screen and the task inspector. `gateway/tasks.py::api_task_diff` is only the ROUTE; every decision about what it answers — both sources, the shared artifact resolver, and the whole git invocation discipline below — lives in `gateway/task_diff.py`, which is pure computation over a task result plus the repo on disk. The worker runs off the event loop (`asyncio.to_thread`) because it reads artifact bytes and shells out to git, and it runs behind a process-wide `asyncio.Semaphore(2)` admission gate so the Changes screen and the inspector asking at once QUEUE instead of fanning out git processes against the owner's repository (queued, never refused for load). `status` is `pending | ready | empty | blocked` derived from the effective task status plus `artifact_status`, and the ONLY 404 is an unknown task id — a running workspace task whose artifacts are not finalized is `pending`, never a 404 and never a fabricated "no changes". A WORKSPACE task's `source` is `workspace_patch`: the patch is the durable `workspace.patch` artifact's bytes, with `workspace_patch.json` supplying `base_commit`/HEAD drift and its `errors[]` as blockers (`ready_no_changes` → `empty`, `failed` → `blocked`), and its `counts.untracked_excluded` / `counts.tracked_excluded` surfaced as the `workspace_paths_excluded` blocker — the capture itself dropped paths, so the patch is complete for what it covers but that is never left unsaid. Artifact lookup, name matching and directory containment live in ONE shared resolver (`task_diff.resolve_task_artifact_path`) used by both this endpoint and `api_task_artifact`, so the traversal guard exists once; a declared-but-escaping path is refused as a typed blocker rather than served. A SELF-REPO task has no historical patch, so `source` is `mutation_baseline`: an honestly-labelled LIVE projection whose candidate set comes from the persisted `terminal_candidate_snapshot` for a terminal task and from `attributed_git_candidates()` while the task runs (the mutation-attribution SSOT — this endpoint never re-parses raw evidence), matched to the ONE `system_repo` baseline surface whose canonical root equals the request's repo dir. The patch is `git diff <base_commit> -- <candidates>` plus a `--no-index` section per attributed untracked file (against the literal `/dev/null`, which is what git itself understands as the empty side — not the host's `os.devnull`). Because this shells out to the owner's real repository while answering a browser read, the invocation discipline is part of the contract: `--no-ext-diff --no-textconv` so a repo-configured external differ can never execute on the host (BIBLE P3); `GIT_LITERAL_PATHSPECS=1` so every candidate path is a LITERAL — candidates are task-attributed filenames, and a task-created file named `:(top)` or `:!secret` would otherwise turn the pathspec into repo-wide MAGIC that puts the owner's own unattributed edits into a patch labelled as the task's work; `base_commit` validated as a hex object name (`[0-9a-fA-F]{7,64}`) BEFORE it reaches argv, because it sits before `--` where git reads `--output=<path>` as an OPTION and would write the patch over a file of the caller's choosing (a non-hex baseline is `blocked` + `base_commit_unknown`); `git ls-files --others --exclude-standard -z` (NUL-separated, `core.quotepath=off`) so a new file named `héllo.txt` is listed as a path that exists rather than the C-quoted string that silently vanishes from the diff, with a listed new file that yielded no diff text disclosed as `untracked_patch_unavailable` (an omission is never "no change"); and git's stdout decoded from BYTES with `errors="replace"`, because a repo may legally hold latin-1 content and an owner-facing read must degrade to a visible replacement character instead of 503-ing the whole Changes screen. Cost is bounded honestly rather than silently: past ~200 untracked sections the untracked projection is dropped as a WHOLE with `untracked_projection_capped` (a prefix of N of M new files reads exactly like a complete diff). The read is BOUND to the state it was computed from: HEAD, THIS WORKTREE'S OWN index stat (a `git add`/`git rm` moves work between index and worktree without touching HEAD or any candidate mtime, and that changes what `git diff <base> -- <path>` reports) and a per-candidate stat fingerprint are taken before and after, a mismatch retries ONCE, and a second mismatch answers `blocked` with `projection_changed_during_read` rather than a patch that does not belong to the disclosed baseline. The index path is asked of git (`rev-parse --git-path index`, resolved once per read and cached across that read's fingerprints), not assumed to be `<root>/.git/index`: in a LINKED worktree `.git` is a FILE, so the assumed path does not exist and the index half of the fingerprint silently collapsed to one constant on every read — a guard that looked present while catching nothing (P1). An index git cannot locate stays the honest absent marker; HEAD and the per-candidate stats still bind the read. Attribution blockers are passed through untouched, and `baseline_stale` — the drift condition itself — is surfaced as the boolean `head_advanced` while the current projection is still shown, because refusing there would hide real work behind an evidence footnote. Consequence worth knowing: a RUNNING self-repo task that already COMMITTED all its work has no live candidate set (the live authority attributes working-tree-dirty paths only), so it answers `blocked` + `baseline_stale` until it terminalizes and its snapshot — which does combine dirty and committed paths — is recorded. There is deliberately NO server-side file-stat block and NO patch truncation: the client parses the same bytes it renders (one snapshot, one truth), and an owner-facing diff is never silently clipped — a patch beyond ~8 MB is REFUSED as `blocked` + `patch_too_large` with an empty patch rather than shortened into something the owner would review as complete. `patch_sha256` is the digest of the SERVED text (utf-8) on BOTH sources, never of the on-disk bytes: digesting the bytes would disagree with the string the owner received whenever a byte had to be replaced during decoding, and would report a healthy transfer as corrupt.

`GET /api/projects/{project_id}/threads/{thread_id}/diff` answers the SAME envelope plus `{project_id, thread_id}` for a branched thread's own checkout (A13/X9). Changes is task-centric and the per-task route structurally cannot answer this: a thread worktree is a PERSISTENT checkout with no task, no artifact and no attributed path set, so `source` is `thread_checkout` and the patch is the WHOLE tree against the `base_sha` recorded on the thread-worktree registry row — committed work, staged work, unsaved edits and untracked new files alike, because that is what the owner sees when they open that folder. It reuses `task_diff.py`'s hardened invocation, its envelope, its no-clipping rule and the SAME process-wide diff gate; forking a second git layer for a second surface is how two answers to one question start disagreeing. Its read-repeat guard differs in one deliberate way: with no candidate list, the path set is derived from `git status --porcelain -z --untracked-files=all` and each named path contributes the same size/mtime row, while the worktree index stat is EXCLUDED — `git status` refreshes the index, and a freshly created checkout is full of racily-clean files, so including the index mtime made the fingerprint differ from itself on every read of a new checkout and refused a diff that was never unstable. A thread that has NOT branched off answers `blocked` + `thread_not_branched` rather than `empty`: it works in the project folder, and "no changes" would be a lie about work that is simply somewhere else. `web/modules/changes.js` carries the matching EXPLICIT source mode (`SOURCE_TASK` / `SOURCE_THREAD`) rather than inferring the source from whichever id happens to be set — including the "nothing selected" question, which is asked of the SOURCE and not of `taskId`, because a thread checkout has none; a thread menu opens it through the `ouro:open-thread-changes` window event (`project_thread_actions.openThreadChanges`), the same seam shape the task inspector already uses, so no menu needs a handle on the Changes controller; selecting one clears the other and a late answer from the source the owner just left is dropped instead of painted under the new header, `thread_not_branched` / `checkout_missing` render as neutral STATE sentences (not "no trustworthy diff"), every other blocked answer keys its NOUN on `source` too ("for this checkout", never "for this task" — a thread worktree has no task to go and look at), non-blocking notes are labelled "Notes on this checkout" instead of naming an attribution mechanism that is not involved, and the "Request edits" handoff names the thread's BRANCH because there is no task id to name.

Diff PARSING is client-side (`web/modules/patch_parse.js`, pure and node-tested): the server returns the raw patch and the browser derives the file list, per-file status (M/A/D/rename/binary) and +/− counts from those exact bytes. One snapshot is one truth — a second server-side stat source could silently disagree with the patch the owner is reading. The parser closes a hunk on the line counts the hunk header itself declared (not on "this line looks like a header"), which is what keeps a DELETED line whose content starts with `--- ` a deletion; it carries no `diff --no-index` branch, because `git diff --no-index -- /dev/null <new>` (how the server projects an untracked file) still emits an ordinary `diff --git a/<new> b/<new>` header — a branch for a string real git never writes would be untested-by-construction code claiming coverage it does not have; split view pairs each deletion run with the following addition run and renders an empty counterpart cell for the shorter side. Nothing is smoothed over: `pending`, `blocked` (with its typed blocker tokens) and HEAD drift each get an explicit banner, and the drift sentence is a boolean disclosure — "HEAD differs from the task baseline; showing the current projection for paths attributed during the task window" — carrying no commit counts and no exclusive-ownership claim (attribution is evidence, not exclusion). Two calibration rules keep those banners worth reading, and both surfaces share one copy of each: drift is disclosed for `source === 'mutation_baseline'` ONLY, because a workspace task's patch is durable bytes taken at its own base and the sentence would describe a repo that patch does not depend on; and a `blocked` answer whose evidence is just `baseline_missing` (or absent entirely) reads as the neutral "No diff baseline was recorded for this task" with no blocker code, because that is the ORDINARY outcome for a task that never touched the repo and alarming copy there teaches the owner to ignore the banner that matters. The screen's only action is "Request edits": the dock is a `composer_parts` instance whose ORDERED parts go to the chat controller's `sendParts` behind a prepended plain-text `Re task <id> ("<title>"): ` line, and the dock clears only after that handoff succeeds. There is deliberately no Approve control (owner-locked scope).

The dock is also a `[data-capture-dock]`, so ⌘L works here exactly as on Files: the global handler (`app.js [anchor:phase-C]`) names the active page in one CANCELABLE `ouro:capture-selection` event, and this page consuming it — `preventDefault()` — is what licenses suppressing the browser default. A diff selection becomes a chip named by the NEW-side line numbers found ANYWHERE in it (min/max, not the boundary rows) with the selected diff lines inlined verbatim, because those are the lines that exist in the file the agent will edit — a selection that merely starts or ends on a `-` row still has a true new-side span, and the deleted lines ride verbatim inside the fence. The range is CLAMPED to the hunk the selection STARTS in, bytes and all, so two rows in hunks 400 lines apart can no longer be published as `L18-L401`; the rows the clamp dropped are disclosed in a toast. A selection with NO new-side number anywhere is a PURE deletion, which no range can honestly name: it is added to the dock as a path-bearing TEXT quote (`<path> — deleted lines (no new-side line numbers):` + the verbatim lines) rather than degrading to a whole-file marker that loses the bytes, and the toast says a quote was added instead of a chip. That path stays RARE by construction — one context or added row in the selection yields a real chip — which matters because a text part carries no remove button of its own. No selection is the whole-file chip. The always-visible "Add selection ⌘L" button is the GUARANTEED path — some browsers keep ⌘L for the address bar — and suppresses its own mousedown default so it cannot destroy the selection it is about to read.

### Files

`web/modules/files.js` is a READ-ONLY code browser with context capture (redesign decision 18): a lazy tree rail (a "Go to file…" filter over what is already listed, one `/api/files/list` call per expanded directory), and a viewer that renders text previews as ONE ROW PER LINE — each row carrying `data-line-number`, the substrate the selection→range mapping resolves against — with syntax colors from `web/modules/code_highlight.js`, plus Download (the desktop host bridge and the web fallback share the one
download contract) and ⌘L context capture. The header states `N lines shown · preview truncated` whenever the payload is a prefix, so the page never claims a total the client cannot know, and it shows no mtime / "modified by" fiction. The editor and its save, new file/directory, upload and the drop overlay, the copy/move clipboard, delete, and the context menu were removed from the UI; with no editor there is no unsaved state, so the page registers no `setBeforePageLeave` guard. The backend write endpoints in `gateway/files.py` are a FROZEN CONTRACT this page simply never calls.

Capture is a SPECIFIED mapping, not a heuristic (`selectionLineRange`, node-tested): boundaries come from `getRangeAt(0)` and are ordered (a backward drag reverses anchor/focus), BOTH boundaries must resolve to `data-line-number` rows of the active viewer, an end boundary at offset 0 of a later line excludes that line, and a collapsed selection captures nothing. An ELEMENT boundary carries a child index rather than a character offset (DOM spec), so the DOM layer normalizes it first (`boundaryBeforeText`): a boundary positioned before the row's code text reads as offset 0 whatever its child index. That normalization is SPEC-HARDENING, not the fix for an observed bug — probing Chromium and WebKit, a drag STARTED over the unselectable line-number gutter never produced a nonzero child-index boundary. The boundary actually observed at the gutter is a drag whose END lands there: it arrives as element offset 0 on the row, which the offset-zero exclusion above already reads correctly. The spec permits a nonzero child index at exactly that position, so the flag keeps both readings identical instead of depending on which index an engine happens to pick. The chip is built through the shared `composer_parts` codec with the canonical absolute `display_path` and the selected lines VERBATIM (the codec itself decides inline-vs-bare-marker); a path the codec cannot round-trip is refused with a visible toast instead of a mangled marker. A range that touches the LAST shown line of a TRUNCATED preview carries no bytes at all (`captureInlinesContent`) — that line may be a fragment the server cut mid-line, so the chip degrades to the ranged bare marker and discloses it once per file. Chips land in the page's OWN bottom dock (a `createComposerParts` instance) so comments can be typed between them; Enter hands the ordered parts to the chat controller's `sendParts`, and navigation to Chat happens only after that handoff succeeds (a failed send keeps the draft). The global ⌘L hotkey lives in the `web/app.js` `[anchor:phase-C]` region: it respects focused editables other than a capture dock (via the shared `isKeyboardEditable`, one selector list for the whole app) and only names the active page in one CANCELABLE `ouro:capture-selection` event, which the page owning the surface listens for. The browser default is suppressed only when a listener actually consumed the event, so with no owning page ⌘L falls through to the browser instead of being swallowed to do nothing. It is best-effort by design — the visible "Add to chat" / "Add selection" buttons are the guaranteed path (decision 10), and the selection button suppresses `mousedown` so clicking it cannot collapse the selection it is about to read; the mouseup cache is readable ONLY by that button and by a ⌘L pressed with focus inside the dock (the "select code, type a comment, then ⌘L" flow), because anywhere else a remembered range would be stale.

v1 limitation, stated plainly: a bare or over-cap context marker names the FILE BROWSER's absolute path, which the agent can read only when that path falls under its own user-files root (project/remote binding is deferred, decision 31); inline-content chips are unaffected, since their bytes travel inside the message itself.

`web/modules/code_highlight.js` is the in-house highlighter (decision 19), XSS-safe by construction: plain text is tokenized into typed lexemes (keyword/self/string/number/comment/default, plus markdown headings), EVERY lexeme is escaped, and only then wrapped in `<span class="tok-…">` — one escape site, no path from source bytes to the DOM that skips it. Languages are keyed by extension (python, markdown, js, json, css, yaml; unknown = escaped plain text) and colors come only from the `--code-*` tokens. It is one regex pass per line with no cross-line parser state, so a construct spanning lines colors its delimiters and leaves the body default.

Backend policy lives in `gateway/files.py`: root confinement is enforced on the RESOLVED path (v6.26.0) — symlinks whose target leaves the configured root are listed but not readable/writable/traversable; owner-only state and skill control-plane sidecars are protected.

Unsaved text is guarded on file selection, directory change, page navigation, and browser unload; Save is available only for a writable complete text read, while a truncated text preview remains read-only. The backend is the path authority. Every file route resolves its requested path under the configured root; a symlink whose target leaves that root may be listed as a symlink but cannot be read, written, deleted, downloaded, or traversed. UI path strings and disabled buttons are presentation only and never replace resolved-path confinement.

### Skills and Widgets

Skills has three views: installed skills, ClawHub, and OuroborosHub. Marketplace panes initialize lazily and refresh installed state when revisited. The installed view merges extension truth with the serialized lifecycle queue so install, update, review, dependency work, enable, disable, repair, uninstall, and failure remain visible while an operation is queued or running instead of snapping back to stale card state.

Installation, deterministic preflight, LLM review, owner grants, dependency readiness, extension loading, enablement, and execution are separate lifecycle facts. A fresh executable review does not imply that requested keys were granted or dependencies installed, and `enabled=true` does not override a blocked review or load error. Owner attestation, where eligible, skips only the expensive LLM review; deterministic preflight and the normal post-pass dependency/extension reconciliation still run. Repair creates a real constrained managed task visible in Chat, and hub submission creates a real supervised task rather than printing a success-shaped chat placebo.

Widgets is a separate page because extension UI is an execution surface, not catalogue metadata. It renders only UI tabs registered by reviewed live extensions and supports three modes. An extension-route iframe uses an empty sandbox capability set. A declarative widget is rendered by host-owned code from a validated schema. A reviewed module widget runs in an opaque-origin `srcdoc` iframe with `allow-scripts` but without `allow-same-origin`.

Declarative widgets support forms and actions, status/data/text/code/markdown, tables, tabs, charts, polls, jobs, streams, subscriptions, progress, media, files, maps, calendars, kanban, and composition through `group`, `metric`, and `callout`. One recursive validator limits the tree to depth 8 and 256 nodes and reports the exact failing path. Nested interactive components use an explicit id or stable tree path as identity; `subscription.render` remains transitively passive so an incoming event cannot smuggle a new active control tree past validation. Text, attributes, links, media routes, and field values are escaped or constrained for their actual sink.

Module widgets receive a narrow parent-mediated fetch bridge. The iframe's policy denies ambient network and origin authority; the parent accepts requests only to the exact owning extension prefix under `/api/extensions/<skill>/...` and returns the response through a nonce-bound message exchange. This preserves useful route I/O without giving reviewed skill JavaScript the SPA's cookies, DOM, or broad API authority. Chart.js is bundled locally, and module/declarative rendering must not depend on a third-party CDN.

A mounted declarative widget owns its timers, abort controllers, chart objects, event streams, polls, jobs, and WebSocket message handlers through one disposer. Leaving Widgets or forcing a refresh disposes the mounted work; a later visit may repaint the last good extension payload and restore bounded widget session state without leaving the hidden copy running. Poll and WebSocket writers use monotonic progress for one job so an older response cannot rewind a newer event. Transient refresh failure preserves the last good widgets instead of blanking the page. Card order is keyboard- and drag-adjustable owner UI state stored through `/api/ui/preferences`; it never rewrites the extension manifest or changes the review boundary.

### Dashboard

Dashboard groups Logs, Evolution, Costs, Updates, and Activity under one page. Sub-tabs activate their own loading and refresh policy instead of running every expensive reader continuously while hidden. Logs and Chat share event summarization (`log_events.js`) so task phases are described consistently. The same `taskOutcomeSeverity` reducer drives both surfaces: a degraded review or best-effort/degraded objective is warning-colored, never presented as a green solved result. Compact review projections disclose every panel and actor's model/provider, role, transport/parse/semantic state, coverage, quorum contribution, reason, and enforcement impact by default; since v6.70.0 the actor/panel `reason` is the COMPLETE redacted rationale (the former 500/800-char caps destroyed the only owner-reachable copy — reviewer rationale is a cognitive artifact, BIBLE P1) and each actor carries a forensic `response_ref` into the private observability store; full model prompts/responses themselves remain in that private audit storage. Evolution reads `/api/evolution-data`; Costs reads the physical-attempt ledger projection from `/api/cost-breakdown` and distinguishes confirmed/settled, reserved, unresolved upper bound, and unknown/unmetered work together with `cost_final`; it exposes budget controls from the shared setup contract via `/api/settings` GET/POST. Updates exposes official managed updates plus local recovery commits/tags.
The Logs page renders task/LLM/tool/progress events as grouped task cards, while Chat renders the same stream as a live task card so operational history and live dialogue stay visually consistent.
Worker tasks forward their `append_jsonl` log lines to the live dashboard over `EVENT_Q` via a per-worker log sink installed in `supervisor/workers.py::worker_main` (the WS log sink only exists in the main process), suppressing types that already arrive through a dedicated live sibling event — `tool_call`/`llm_round`/`task_checkpoint`/`task_done`/`llm_usage` — to avoid double broadcast and a double `task_checkpoint` file write. `EVENT_Q` belongs to the `server.py` process, not to a replaceable worker-pool generation: direct chat, consciousness, active turns, and every respawned worker retain one manager-backed queue proxy until process exit. The manager transport serializes in the producer and isolates producer connections, avoiding both feeder-thread false positives and pipe corruption when a worker is force-killed mid-frame. `spawn_workers` therefore reuses that bus, holds the lifecycle lock through complete pool publication, and refuses to overwrite a non-empty live pool; single-slot recovery uses `respawn_worker`. On load and on every reconnect the Logs page backfills recent history from `/api/logs/{events,tools,progress,supervisor}` (`web/modules/logs.js::backfillRecentLogs`) and dedupes the live-overlap window by event identity so the pre-connect window is neither dropped nor shown twice.
Chart.js is bundled locally as `web/chart.umd.min.js`; no CDN dependency by design. Because a canvas cannot resolve CSS `var(...)`, `web/modules/utils.js::readThemeTokens` is the single canvas↔token boundary: the chart-theme helpers `evolution.js::evolutionChartTheme` and `widgets.js::chartPalette` (with `utils.js::chartColorAlpha` for the dataset fills — it sits beside `readThemeTokens` because both chart modules share it, rather than Widgets importing a colour utility from the Evolution page) resolve every series colour, axis colour and font stack off the live `:root` computed style at chart-build time, so no chart holds a hand-synced copy of the design tokens — and every token name they pass must be DEFINED in `:root`, because `getPropertyValue` returns the COMPUTED value: an alias such as `--accent-system: var(--amber)` resolves through to the hex and is safe to name, while an UNDEFINED custom property returns the empty string, which a canvas accepts and then silently drops the series for (pinned by `tests/test_widgets_ui_static.py::test_canvas_palettes_read_only_defined_root_tokens`).

Logs merges live WebSocket log frames with bounded REST backfill from events, tools, progress, and supervisor logs. It orders the merged backfill chronologically, deduplicates overlap with the live stream and reconnect backfills, groups related task events into bounded cards, exposes the raw record on demand, and applies shared category/severity/review presentation from `log_events.js`. Clearing the visible panel does not delete the underlying logs.

Activity shows running and pending queue entries, background-consciousness state, and scheduled work. It offers mechanical controls only where that surface is authoritative: typed cascade cancellation for a live task, start/stop for background consciousness, and enable/disable/delete for owner-managed schedules. A schedule reconciled from a skill manifest is shown read-only as managed by that skill because a direct edit here would be overwritten by the skill lifecycle and would falsely appear durable.

Costs is a projection of the physical-attempt ledger. It distinguishes confirmed, reserved, unresolved upper-bound, unknown or unmetered usage, open rows, and finality; unavailable data renders as unavailable rather than `$0`. Breakdowns by model, key, model category, and task category remain views over the same ledger. Total budget can hot-apply, while the per-task value is labelled and treated as a hard cost cap over the whole root tree for the next task, not as the obsolete own-task soft warning. Increasing a cap does not automatically resume work that already finalized or paused.

Evolution shows current evolution and background-consciousness state, campaign objective/progress, queue/failure/budget information, and durable evolution history. Starting a campaign uses the shared input dialog: cancellation starts nothing, confirmed empty input selects the backend's default autonomous objective, and entered text becomes the objective. Light runtime mode disables self-modifying campaigns rather than presenting a control that the backend will refuse.

Updates separates passive status from explicit mutation. Opening the page reads cached/local update and git state without fetching the network. Check performs the explicit remote refresh; Update runs preflight and applies the returned exact plan; conflicting work may enter the reviewed assisted path; Recovery is a separately confirmed replace action that first preserves rescue/keep references. Local commits and tags support explicit restore, and promotion is explicitly confirmed. The UI must surface unavailable, divergent, dirty, unsafe, failed-check, rollback, and restart-required states instead of reducing them to one enabled button.

### Settings and onboarding

Settings has Providers, Secrets, Models, Behavior, Advanced, and About tabs. Providers configures remote providers, custom compatible endpoints, local runtime entry points, the optional non-loopback network gate, and Harness Accounts. Secrets centralizes known provider/integration secrets, skill-requested keys, and owner-defined custom keys without returning stored secret values. Models contains ordinary model slots, effort lanes, reviewer slots, and subagent routing. Behavior contains owner choices such as context, safety-supervisor coverage, task acceptance, self-evolution, and prompt-cache posture. Advanced contains process, timeout, local-model, integration, source-control, and cleanup controls. About reports the current application/runtime identity.

Desktop onboarding and the blocking web overlay render the same provider, model, review/runtime, budget, and summary steps and save through the same backend normalization; context mode remains a separate owner setting. Startup readiness is structural: a recognized non-empty remote configuration or a task-capable local-routing flag is sufficient. Credential validity, entitlement, model availability, and local-process health remain runtime status, not onboarding admission. Linux browser fallback therefore does not maintain a second setup flow.

Harness Accounts is the owner-facing projection of Ouroboros's owned Claudexor daemon. The browser never receives its control token or interprets credentials. Status combines daemon/runtime readiness, login-capable harness discovery, credential profiles, honest vendor-live versus local-session verification, fresh quota windows, and optional model discovery. API-key-only adapters do not acquire fake Login buttons merely because they appear in a broader execution catalogue.

Connect is link-first and harness-agnostic. A typed disclosure renders the sign-in URL and any one-time code; flows that may need a pasted callback code keep that optional field visible while active because the browser callback may complete without it. An old engine may expose the copy-paste attach command only as a collapsed Advanced fallback in the owner's own terminal. There is no embedded terminal login surface. Terminal job state and the current account row are reconciled so a stale verification read during login cannot claim failure after the account actually connected.

Account status refresh runs immediately and on visible page/tab activation but does not make every hidden page pay for daemon round-trips. Job polling uses one request at a time, begins at the healthy cadence, backs off to a bounded delay on consecutive failures, and after ten consecutive failures stops with an honest unconfirmed state: lost contact does not prove either failure or settlement. One transition lock covers Start, Retry, and Dismiss. A new login begins only after the prior snapshot is terminal or cancellation is proven by success, 404, or 410; a network or server failure retains the card and job id because dropping it could orphan a still-live server job. After each await the handler rechecks whether polling settled the job, so a stale cancel continuation cannot overwrite a terminal result.

Reviewer Slots edits one structured reviewer configuration. Each triad, scope, or optional advisory row chooses either API delivery or a coding-agent session, then its model, optional credential profile, and effort. API models use free text with catalogue suggestions; agent-session models come from the selected harness. Saved choices that disappear from discovery remain visible as unavailable rather than silently changing to the first option. Capability labels configure nothing; the server-returned limits and last effective execution disclose what a saved row actually ran as, including capability deltas. An unloaded or unreachable view authors no replacement. A successfully loaded empty triad/scope is sent as shown so backend validation returns the real error instead of the browser falsely reporting that nothing changed.

The Subagents section distinguishes an ordinary API child from delegated coding on a connected subscription. Delegation selects the harness and an optional engine model, while the API-side subagent still drives, observes, and checks the delegated session. The latest settled delegated run is shown from durable evidence, with requested-versus-applied model differences disclosed rather than rewritten. No connected account yields an explanatory state, not a toggle whose every dispatch silently falls back.

Mutative subagents use Off, Auto, and On. An explicit Off or On applies to every acting surface. Auto delegates the default to runtime mode: Advanced and Pro allow worktree, external-workspace, and genesis writers; Light allows only children that build outside the Ouroboros runtime (external workspace and genesis) and keeps a self-worktree child off. Read-only children remain available. The setting is owner-controlled, applies from the next task, and is independent of whether the execution route is API or a coding-agent subscription.

Prompt Cache TTL is one global owner choice: provider default, five minutes, or one hour. It applies to every lane rather than letting task, review, and safety builders drift into conflicting cache horizons. The provider-send finalizer applies the choice only to existing cache markers on compatible Anthropic-family payloads; the UI does not promise cache behavior on providers that manage it implicitly. The shipped one-hour posture favors reuse across long waits and review cycles; choosing another value takes effect from the next task.

Settings save classifies effects rather than claiming that every value became live at once. Total budget, tool timeout, GitHub metadata, and update channel hot-apply; the retained soft/hard timeout keys are accepted only as deprecated audited no-ops. Ordinary models, credentials, efforts, reviewer/subagent configuration, per-task cost cap, safety posture, and prompt-cache TTL apply from the next task; a running task keeps its starting snapshot and the response says so. Worker count, bind host, local-model runtime, provider base/runtime parameters, and background-consciousness timing require restart. Runtime mode and context mode keep dedicated owner paths because generic settings writes must not silently lower authority or cognitive/review posture. Backend failure and browser transport failure remain distinct; absence of a successful status read is never evidence that a runtime is healthy.

### Visual verification policy

A visible change is exercised in at least one relevant real consumer flow and the rendered result is inspected with vision. A saved screenshot alone is not verification.
